lesson as a self-contained review preview.\"\"\"\n+from pathlib import Path\n+import html\n+import json\n+import re\n+\n+HERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent\n+RECAP = HERE.parents[1] / \"students/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md\"\n+DRAFT = HERE / \"concept-lesson-v3.md\"\n+OUTPUT = HERE / \"conditional-reasoning-v3.html\"\n+BEGIN = \"<!-- BEGIN CURRENT STUDENT LESSON V3 -->\"\n+END = \"<!-- END CURRENT STUDENT LESSON V3 -->\"\n+\n+\n+def source_text():\n+    canonical = RECAP.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8-sig\")\n+    if BEGIN in canonical or END in canonical:\n+        if canonical.count(BEGIN) != 1 or canonical.count(END) != 1:\n+            raise ValueError(\"Current lesson markers must appear exactly once\")\n+        text = canonical.split(BEGIN, 1)[1].split(END, 1)[0].strip()\n+        origin = \"canonical current student lesson V3 marker block\"\n+    else:\n+        text = DRAFT.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8-sig\").strip()\n+        origin = \"concept-lesson-v3.md review draft\"\n+    if \"Notes for me\" in text or \"teacher_note\" in text:\n+        raise ValueError(\"Private material found in student lesson\")\n+    return text, origin\n+\n+\n+def inline(text):\n+    text = html.escape(text)\n+    text = re.sub(r\"\\*\\*([^*]+)\\*\\*\", r\"<strong>\\1</strong>\", text)\n+    return re.sub(r\"`([^`]+)`\", r\"<code>\\1</code>\", text)\n+\n+\n+def route(nodes, label=\"Rule diagram\"):\n+    return '<div class=\"route\" role=\"group\" aria-label=\"'+html.escape(label)+'\">'+\"<span class=\\\"arrow\\\" aria-hidden=\\\"true\\\">→</span>\".join('<span class=\"node\">'+inline(n)+'</span>' for n in nodes)+\"</div>\"\n+\n+\n+FACT_LAB = '''<section class=\"fact-lab\" aria-label=\"Explore what a fact lets you conclude\"><div class=\"lab-kicker\">A little experiment</div><h2>Change the fact.</h2><p class=\"lab-rule\">Every late application requires special approval.</p><label for=\"fact-choice\">What do we know about this application?</label><select id=\"fact-choice\"><option value=\"late\">It was submitted late.</option><option value=\"notapproval\">It does not require special approval.</option><option value=\"approval\">It requires special approval.</option><option value=\"notlate\">It was not submitted late.</option></select><div class=\"fact-display\" aria-live=\"polite\"><span class=\"eyebrow\" id=\"fact-move\">Apply forward</span><p id=\"fact-result\">It requires special approval.</p><p id=\"fact-reason\">The fact meets the rule's starting condition. The requirement follows.</p></div><p class=\"lab-foot\">One rule. Four different facts. Two support a new conclusion.</p></section>'''\n+\n+VISUALS = {\n+    \"TWO-MOVES\": '<figure class=\"two-moves\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">01 · Start at the condition</span>'+route([\"Submitted late\", \"Requires approval\"])+\"<p>A late application cannot escape the approval requirement.</p></div><div><span class=\\\"eyebrow\\\">02 · Deny the required result</span>\"+route([\"Does not require approval\", \"Not submitted late\"])+\"<p>If the result is absent, the starting condition cannot hold.</p></div><figcaption>Two directions of reasoning. One unchanged policy.</figcaption></figure>\",\n+    \"CASE-GRID\": '''<figure class=\"case-grid\"><div class=\"case-heading\"><span>Submitted late?</span><span>Requires approval?</span><span>Fits the rule?</span></div><div><span>Yes</span><span>Yes</span><b>Allowed</b></div><div class=\"impossible\"><span>Yes</span><span>No</span><b>Ruled out</b></div><div><span>No</span><span>Yes</span><b>Allowed</b></div><div><span>No</span><span>No</span><b>Allowed</b></div><figcaption>A rule rules out a combination. It does not establish which allowed combination actually occurs.</figcaption></figure>''',\n+    \"CHAIN-LAB\": '<section class=\"chain-lab\" aria-label=\"Explore which rule can link\"><span class=\"lab-kicker\">Match the middle</span><h3>Can these rules form a chain?</h3><div class=\"chain-first\">'+route([\"Submitted late\", \"Requires special approval\"])+'''</div><label for=\"chain-choice\">Choose the second rule</label><select id=\"chain-choice\"><option value=\"match\">Requires special approval → Needs a signature</option><option value=\"received\">Has received special approval → Needs a signature</option><option value=\"shared\">Submitted late → Needs a signature</option><option value=\"reversed\">Needs a signature → Requires special approval</option></select><div id=\"chain-display\" aria-live=\"polite\"></div><p class=\"lab-foot\">The destination of one rule must match the starting condition of the next.</p></section>''',\n+    \"SHARED-TRIGGER\": '<figure class=\"fork\"><span class=\"fork-root\">Submitted late</span><div class=\"fork-lines\" aria-hidden=\"true\">↙ &nbsp; ↘</div><div class=\"fork-results\"><span>Requires approval</span><span>Needs a signature</span></div><figcaption>Both results follow for a late application. The diagram supplies no arrow between the results.</figcaption></figure>',\n+    \"MODALITY\": '''<figure class=\"word-contrast\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">What the rule says</span><strong>Should be inspected</strong><small>A claim about what ought to happen.</small></div><span class=\"not-equal\" aria-label=\"does not mean\">≠</span><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">What an answer might add</span><strong>Has been inspected</strong><small>A claim about what actually happened.</small></div><figcaption>Dropping one word can change the whole condition.</figcaption></figure>''',\n+    \"NEGATION\": '''<figure class=\"negation\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">To fail an AND requirement</span><div class=\"logic-parts\"><span>Not reviewed</span><b>OR</b><span>Not authorized</span></div><p>At least one required part is missing.</p></div><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">To fail an OR requirement</span><div class=\"logic-parts\"><span>No badge</span><b>AND</b><span>No invitation</span></div><p>Every permitted route is missing.</p></div><figcaption>Negate each part and switch the connector.</figcaption></figure>''',\n+}\n+\n+\n+class Renderer:\n+    def __init__(self):\n+        self.checks = 0\n+        self.practice = []\n+\n+    def check(self, prompt, answer):\n+        self.checks += 1\n+        n = self.checks\n+        return f'''<aside class=\"check\" data-check=\"{n}\"><span class=\"eyebrow\">Try it · {n:02}</span><p>{inline(prompt)}</p><form><label for=\"answer-{n}\">Your answer and reasoning</label><textarea id=\"answer-{n}\" rows=\"3\" required placeholder=\"Write what follows, and why.\"></textarea><button type=\"submit\">Compare with the worked answer <span aria-hidden=\"true\">↗</span></button></form><div class=\"feedback\" hidden tabindex=\"-1\"><span class=\"eyebrow\">Worked answer</span><p>{inline(answer)}</p><p class=\"small\">Your response stays above. Compare the conclusion and the reasoning. This exercise does not automatically grade your writing.</p><button class=\"again\" type=\"button\">Edit my answer</button></div><noscript><p><strong>Worked answer:</strong> {inline(answer)}</p></noscript></aside>'''\n+\n+    def prose(self, block):\n+        if block.startswith(\"|\"):\n+            rows = [[v.strip() for v in line.strip().strip(\"|\").split(\"|\")] for line in block.splitlines()]\n+            rows = [r for r in rows if not all(re.fullmatch(r\":?-+:?\", v) for v in r)]\n+            return '<div class=\"table-scroll\"><table><thead><tr>'+\"\".join('<th scope=\"col\">'+inline(v)+\"</th>\" for v in rows[0])+\"</tr></thead><tbody>\"+\"\".join(\"<tr>\"+\"\".join(\"<td>\"+inline(v)+\"</td>\" for v in r)+\"</tr>\" for r in rows[1:])+\"</tbody></table></div>\"\n+        if block.startswith(\"**\") and \" -> \" in block:\n+            label, value = block.split(\":**\", 1)\n+            nodes = value.strip().rstrip(\".\").split(\" -> \")\n+            return '<div class=\"rule-line\"><span class=\"eyebrow\">'+inline(label.strip(\"*\"))+\"</span>\"+route(nodes)+\"</div>\"\n+        return \"<p>\"+inline(block).replace(\"\\n\", \" \")+\"</p>\"\n+\n+    def render(self, source):\n+        blocks = re.split(r\"\\n\\s*\\n\", source)\n+        title = blocks.pop(0).removeprefix(\"# \")\n+        opening = []\n+        while blocks and not blocks[0].startswith(\"## \"):\n+            b = blocks.pop(0)\n+            if b == \"<!-- FACT-LAB -->\":\n+                continue\n+            opening.append(self.prose(b))\n+        body = '<header class=\"opening\"><div class=\"opening-story\">'+\"\".join(opening)+\"</div>\"+FACT_LAB+\"</header>\"\n+        body += '<nav class=\"jump\" aria-label=\"Lesson sections\"><a href=\"#section-1\">Facts</a><a href=\"#section-2\">Contrapositives</a><a href=\"#section-3\">Linking</a><a href=\"#section-5\">Whole ideas</a><a href=\"#section-8\">Practice</a></nav>'\n+        section = False\n+        i = 0\n+        while i < len(blocks):\n+            b = blocks[i].strip()\n+            if b.startswith(\"## \"):\n+                if section:\n+                    body += \"</section>\"\n+                number, heading = b[3:].split(\" | \", 1)\n+                body += f'<section class=\"chapter\" id=\"section-{int(number)}\"><h2><span>{number}</span>'+inline(heading)+\"</h2>\"\n+                section = True\n+            elif b.startswith(\"### \"):\n+                body += \"<h3>\"+inline(b[4:])+\"</h3>\"\n+            elif b.startswith(\"<!-- \"):\n+                name = b.removeprefix(\"<!-- \").removesuffix(\" -->\")\n+                if name not in VISUALS:\n+                    raise ValueError(\"Unknown visual hook: \"+name)\n+                body += VISUALS[name]\n+            elif i+1 < len(blocks) and blocks[i+1].startswith(\"**Worked answer:**\"):\n+                answer = blocks[i+1].removeprefix(\"**Worked answer:**\").strip()\n+                if b.startswith(\"**Practice \"):\n+                    prompt = re.sub(r\"^\\*\\*Practice \\d+:\\*\\*\\s*\", \"\", b)\n+                    self.practice.append({\"prompt\": prompt, \"answer\": answer})\n+                    if len(self.practice) == 1:\n+                        body += '<section class=\"practice\" id=\"mixed-practice\" aria-label=\"Mixed practice\"><div class=\"practice-head\"><span class=\"eyebrow\">Use the moves</span><span id=\"practice-count\">1 of 4</span></div><h3>What follows?</h3><div class=\"practice-columns\"><p id=\"practice-prompt\"></p><form id=\"practice-form\"><label for=\"practice-answer\">Your conclusion and reasoning</label><textarea id=\"practice-answer\" rows=\"5\" required placeholder=\"Name the move. Explain what the statements guarantee.\"></textarea><button type=\"submit\">Compare reasoning</button></form></div><div id=\"practice-feedback\" hidden tabindex=\"-1\"><span class=\"eyebrow\">Worked answer</span><p id=\"practice-worked\"></p><p class=\"small\">Compare for yourself. There is no automatic grading of your explanation.</p></div><div class=\"practice-actions\"><button type=\"button\" id=\"practice-edit\" hidden>Edit my answer</button><button type=\"button\" id=\"practice-next\" hidden>Next problem →</button></div></section>'\n+                    body += '<noscript><p>'+inline(prompt)+\"</p><p><strong>Worked answer:</strong> \"+inline(answer)+\"</p></noscript>\"\n+                else:\n+                    body += self.check(re.sub(r\"^\\*\\*Try it:\\*\\*\\s*\", \"\", b), answer)\n+                i += 1\n+            elif b.startswith(\"**Worked answer:**\"):\n+                raise ValueError(\"Unpaired answer\")\n+            else:\n+                body += self.prose(b)\n+            i += 1\n+        if section:\n+            body += \"</section>\"\n+        return title, body\n+\n+\n+CSS = A fact tells you whether you have reached that condition. Put them together, and you can prove something new.\n+A rule tells you what follows **if a condition is met**. A fact tells you what is true in a particular case. Put them together, and you can prove something new.\n@@\n When an inference seems tempting, try to build a case in which the statements are true and the proposed conclusion is false. If you can, the conclusion is not guaranteed. The counterexamples above work this way: an on-time application can still require approval; a requirement can remain unfulfilled; a trained person can be outside the panel.\n+\n+Keep the scope of the fact too. **Some applications were submitted late. Every late application requires special approval. Therefore, some applications require special approval.** We cannot change “some” to “all.” The rule covers every late case, but the fact establishes only at least one. Other applications could be on time and require no special approval.\n*** End Patch"));

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"7b9c75","wall_time_seconds":2.2007637,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2365,"output":"---\\r\\nname: frontend-design\\r\\ndescription: Guidance for distinctive, intentional visual design when building new UI or reshaping an existing one. Helps with aesthetic direction, typography, and making choices that don\'t read as templated defaults.\\r\\nlicense: Complete terms in LICENSE.txt\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Frontend Design\\r\\n\\r\\nApproach this as the design lead at a design studio known for giving every client a distinct visual identity that is not mistaken for anyone else\'s. This client has already rejected proposals that felt cliché or templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view: make deliberate, opinionated choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this brief, and take aesthetic risk if justified.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ground your designs in the subject matter\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the brief does not identify what the product or subject matter is, identify it yourself before designing, and confirm with the client. You can come up with one concrete subject, the design\'s audience, and the design\'s primary job, as a proposal. If there\'s any information in your memory about the client\'s preferences or context about what they\'re building, use that as a hint. The subject\'s industry, subject matter, materials, and vernacular are where distinctive visual choices come from — a design for a toy for girls aged 8–11 will be very aesthetically different from a dashboard for financial analysts. Build with the brief\'s real content and subject matter throughout.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design principles\\r\\n\\r\\nFor web designs, the hero is the first thing viewers will see. Open with the most characteristic thing in the subject\'s world, in the form that is most appropriate: a headline, an image, an animation, a live demo, an interactive moment, or other treatments. Be deliberate with your choice: a big number with a small label, supporting stats, and a gradient accent is the default treatment, so only use it if that\'s truly the best option.\\r\\n\\r\\nTypography carries the personality of the page. You don\'t need a different typeface for display or headline text and body content: use one family or two, and if two, make them clearly distinct.\\r\\n\\r\\nChoose your typefaces deliberately, not the default families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale following the default guidance of The Elements of Typographic Style with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. When type is used as a headline or visual element, use the type treatment itself as an active part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content.\\r\\n\\r\\nDefault to line lengths of less than 80 characters. Serif typefaces can have slightly longer line lengths; give serif body text slightly more line-height than a sans-serif.\\r\\n\\r\\nAvoid these default typographic treatments; they are the commonest tells of a generated page:\\r\\n- Accenting just a single word or phrase in a headline, like putting one word in italic/bold or a different color.\\r\\n- Using all caps for labels.\\r\\n- Adding unnecessary typographic labels above content.\\r\\n\\r\\nVisual structure is information. Structural devices like outlines, borders, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, etc., encode useful information about the content rather than decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that\'s only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence — like a stepped process or a timeline. Before adding numbered markers, check the content really is a sequence.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse non-user-triggered motion sparingly and deliberately, only to draw attention. A single orchestrated moment — one page-load sequence or one reveal — lands better than scattered effects; fade-and-slide-up entrances on each section and hover transitions on every card are the generic default and read as AI-generated. Motion that answers a person\'s action (opening, expanding, confirming) is welcome when it shows what changed.\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider written content carefully. Often a design brief may not contain real content, and it\'s up to you to come up with copy and placeholder content. Copy can make a design feel as templated as the design itself. See the below section on writing for more guidance.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process: plan, review against the brief, build, critique\\r\\n\\r\\nFor calibration, AI-generated design right now clusters around some traits:\\r\\n1. a warm cream background (near #F4F1EA) with a high-contrast serif display and a terracotta or warm-clay accent (often near #D97757 — Anthropic\'s own Codex-interaction accent, so on a user\'s brief it reads as a tell);\\r\\n2. a near-black background with a single bright acid-green or vermilion accent;\\r\\n3. a broadsheet-style layout with hairline rules, zero border-radius, and dense newspaper-like columns;\\r\\n4. the SaaS-card kit: content chopped into identical rounded cards, one border-radius on everything regardless of hierarchy, the same soft grey shadow (rgba(0,0,0,.1)) under each, and gradient washes as decoration;\\r\\n5. template chrome that appears whatever the subject: a tracked-out ALL-CAPS eyebrow label above every heading; meta strings joined with middle dots (\'A · B · C\'); labels built as \'WORD — fragment\' with a spaced em dash; tinted near-black (#0B0B0B, #111) standing in for black; a monospace face for small data labels; a \'→\' appended to link and button text.\\r\\n\\r\\nAll traits are legitimate for some briefs, but they are defaults rather than choices, and they appear regardless of subject. Where the brief pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — the brief\'s own words always win, including when it asks for one of these looks. Where it leaves an axis free, don\'t spend that freedom on one of these defaults. As with a hired human designer, there\'s often a careful balance between doing what you\'re good at and taking each project as a chance to experiment and learn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWork in two passes. First, brainstorm a short design plan based on the client\'s design brief: create a compact token system with color, type, layout, and principles.\\r\\n- Color: describe the core base palette as 4–6 named hex values.\\r\\n- Type: the typefaces and their roles.\\r\\n- Layout: a layout concept, using one-sentence prose descriptions and ASCII wireframes to ideate and compare. Include alignment guidance; should the content be left aligned, center aligned, justified?\\r\\n- Principles: the high-level guidance for what makes this page unique.\\r\\n\\r\\nThen review that plan against the brief before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page (work through a similar prompt to see if you arrive somewhere similar) rather than a choice made for this specific brief — revise that part, say what you changed and why. Only after you\'ve confirmed the relative uniqueness of your design plan should you start to write the code, following the revised plan.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen writing the code, be careful of structuring your CSS selector specificities. It\'s easy to generate CSS classes that cancel each other out (especially with a type-based selector like .section and an element-based selector like .cta). This can happen often with padding/margin between sections.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Restraint and self-critique\\r\\n\\r\\nSpend your boldness in one place. Let one element be the memorable thing, keep everything around it quiet and disciplined, and cut any decoration that does not serve the brief. Build to a quality floor without announcing it: responsive down to mobile, visible keyboard focus, reduced motion respected, visually accessible, harmonious color palettes. Critique your own work as you build, taking screenshots to review if your environment supports it — a picture is worth 1000 tokens. Consider Chanel\'s advice: before leaving the house, take a look in the mirror and remove one accessory. Human creatives have memory and always try to do something new, so if you have a space to quickly jot down notes about what you\'ve tried, it can help you in future passes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## More on writing in design\\r\\n\\r\\nWords appear in a design for one reason: to make it easier to understand and use. They are design content, not decoration. Bring the same intentionality and minimalism to copywriting that you would bring to spacing and color. Before writing anything, ask what the design needs to say, and how it can best be said to help the person navigate the experience.\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite from the end user\'s perspective. Name things by what users will understand in simple language, not by how the system is built. A user manages notifications, not webhook config. Describe what something is or does in plain terms rather than selling it. Being specific and legible to new users is always better than being clever.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse active voice as default. A CTA says exactly what happens when it is used: \\"Save changes,\\" not \\"Submit.\\" An action keeps the same name through the whole flow, so the button that says \\"Publish\\" produces a toast that says \\"Published.\\" The vocabulary of an interface is the signposting for someone navigating the product. Cohesion and consistency are how people learn their way around.\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat failure and emptiness as moments for direction, not mood. Explain what went wrong and how to fix it, in the interface\'s voice rather than a person\'s. Errors don\'t apologize, and they are never vague about what happened. An empty screen is an invitation to act.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the tone conversational: plain verbs, sentence case, no filler, with tone matched to the brand and the audience. Let each written element do exactly one job.\\r\\n"}'}]

DRAFT.read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig').strip()\n+        origin = 'concept-lesson-v4.md review draft'\n+    if any(value in text for value in ['Notes for me', 'teacher_note', '\\u2014']):\n+ class=\"quiz-verdict\"></strong><p>{html.escape(answer)}</p></div></section>'''\n+\n+\n+base.FACT_LAB = '''<section class=\"fact-lab\" aria-label=\"Explore the fact, rule, and result\"><div class=\"lab-top\"><h2>Follow the fact.</h2><button type=\"button\" id=\"replay-flow\">Replay the path</button></div><label for=\"fact-choice\">Give the application one fact</label><select Then find what follows.\n\n**Fact:** A fact tells you what is true in a particular case.\n**Example:** This application was submitted after the deadline.\n\n**Rule:** A rule tells you what must follow if its condition is met.\n**Example:** Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.\n\n**Conclusion:** A conclusion is what the fact and rule prove together.\n**Example:** This application requires special approval.\n\nThe fact meets the rule's condition. text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re\np=Path('brainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap')\ns=(p/'concept-lesson-v4.md').read_text(encoding='utf-8')\nparts=re.split(r'^## (\\d{2}) \\| (.+)\\n',s,flags=re.M)\nsections={int(parts[i]):parts[i+2].strip() for i in range(1,len(parts),3)}\nopening='''# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\n\nTreat the given statements as true. Your job is to find what else must be true.\n\nBefore you combine statements, identify what each one says. A **fact** describes a case. A **rule** tells you what must follow if its condition is met.\n\nA **conclusion** is what the statements prove. It can follow from a fact and a rule, or from a valid chain of rules.\n'''\nfacts='''A fact tells you what is true in a particular case. **“Application J was submitted after the deadline”** tells us something about Application J. It does not tell us when other applications were submitted.\n\nA fact can also describe more than one case. **“Some applications were submitted after the deadline”** establishes that at least one late application exists. It does not establish that every application was late.\n\nFor now, keep those claims separate from anything you might infer. “J was late” is given. Whether J requires special approval is a different claim. We need a rule connecting lateness to that requirement before we can prove it.\n\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\n\nKeep the whole description. “Requires special approval” is different from “has received special approval.” Each could be a fact about an application, but a requirement does not establish that it has been fulfilled.\n'''\nrules='''A rule tells you what must follow if its condition is met. Consider this policy: **Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.**\n\nThe rule has two complete parts. **Submitted after the deadline** is the condition. **Requires special approval** is the required result. The rule connects them in that order.\n\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\n\nLet **L** mean “submitted after the deadline” and **R** mean “requires special approval.” Then we can write the same rule as **L → R**. The arrow points from the condition to the required result. If L is true, R must be true.\n\nBeing late is **sufficient** to establish the approval requirement. That means lateness is enough to trigger this rule. Requiring approval is **necessary** for a late application. That means a late application cannot lack that requirement.\n\nThose words describe this connection. They do not say that approval is the only requirement, or that lateness is the only reason approval could be required.\n\nThe rule alone does not establish that any application was late. It tells us what is required of any late application, even if no applications were submitted late.\n\n### Keep the kind of claim intact\n\n'''+sections[5]\napply='''Now combine a rule with a fact that meets its condition.\n\n**Rule:** Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.\n\n**Fact:** Application J was submitted after the deadline.\n\n**Conclusion:** Application J requires special approval.\n\nThe fact supplies the condition. The rule supplies the connection to the result. Together, they prove the conclusion.\n\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\n\n'''+sections[1][sections[1].index('**Try it:**'):]+ '\\n\\n'+sections[2]\n# The whole-condition check from chapter1 stays; introduction has already taught notation.\nordered=[('Facts state what is true',facts),('Rules state a requirement',rules),('Apply one rule to a fact',apply),('Negate the whole requirement',sections[6]),('Connect rules at a matching middle',sections[3]),('Shared beginnings do not create a chain',sections[4]),('Choose the move and test the claim',sections[7]),('Mix the moves',sections[8]),('Take this into your assigned work',sections[9])]\nresult=opening+'\\n\\n'+'\\n\\n'.join(f'## {n:02} | {title}\\n\\n{body}' for n,(title,body) in enumerate(ordered,1))+'\\n'\nresult=result.replace(' -> ',' → ').replace('`','')\n(p/'concept-lesson-v5.md').write_text(result,encoding='utf-8')\nb=(p/'build-preview-v4.py').read_text(encoding='utf-8').replace('V4','V5').replace('v4','v5')\n(p/'build-preview-v5.py').write_text(b,encoding='utf-8')\nprint('V5 source reordered, all six checks/four mixed problems preserved, V5 builder seed saved.')\n'@ | python -",max_output_tokens:2000}));

Your job is to find what else must be true.','Conditional reasoning uses rules to work out what must follow from the information given.\\n\\nTreat the given statements as true. Your job is to find what else must be true.',1)\ns=s.replace('<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->','''<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\n\n**Try it:** Some reports are missing pages. Does at least one report with missing pages exist? Must every report be missing pages?\n\n**Worked answer:** At least one report is missing pages. The statement establishes that such a report exists, but does not establish that every report is missing pages. Other reports could be complete.''',1)\ns=s.replace('<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\n\\n','',1)\ns=s.replace('<!-- CASE-GRID -->','<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\n\\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->',1)\nnegative='If it is not true that an item should be inspected, it cannot be damaged under this rule. '\ns=s.replace(negative,'',1)\nanchor='The contrapositive states the same rule from its other end. Reverse the direction **and negate both complete ideas**. The original policy never said that.'\ns=s.replace(anchor,anchor+'\\n\\nKeep any qualifier inside the condition when you negate it. text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re\np=Path('brainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap')\ns=(p/'concept-lesson-v5.md').read_text(encoding='utf-8')\nparts=re.split(r'^## (\\d{2}) \\| (.+)\\n',s,flags=re.M)\nsections={int(parts[i]):parts[i+2].strip() for i in range(1,len(parts),3)}\nchecks=re.findall(r'\\*\\*Try it:\\*\\*.*?\\n\\n\\*\\*Worked answer:\\*\\*.*?(?=\\n\\n|\\Z)',s,flags=re.S)\nassert len(checks)==7\nopening='''# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\n\nConditional reasoning uses general rules to work out what must follow from the information given.\n\nFirst, understand each statement. Then decide whether you are **applying a rule to a case** or **linking rules to make a new rule**. Those moves produce different kinds of conclusions.\n\nIn an inference question, accept all the given statements as true. Your job is to prove what else must be true, without adding a fact or a connection the statements never supplied.\n'''\nfacts='''**Definition:** A fact is information established about a particular case or group. A fact given in the question is treated as true. A fact you infer must be proved from the given information.\n\n**Concrete example:** “Mira is trained.” That tells us something about Mira. It does not tell us whether other people are trained, whether Mira has a badge, or how training relates to badges.\n\nA fact can be negative: **“Niko does not have a badge.”** It can describe a group: **“Some staff members are trained.”** That last statement establishes at least one trained staff member, without establishing that every staff member is trained.\n\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\n\n'''+checks[0]+'''\n\nDo not confuse “fact” with “anything true” when sorting these statements. A rule is also accepted as true when it is given. The distinction is what the statement establishes: a fact supplies information about a case or group; a conditional rule supplies a general connection between conditions.\n\n“Requires approval” and “has received approval” are different facts. A requirement can be established without establishing that it was fulfilled. Keep the complete claim when you read or shorten it.\n'''\nrules='''**Definition:** A conditional rule is a general relationship: every case meeting its starting condition must also meet its result condition. A rule connects conditions; it does not identify a particular case that meets them.\n\n**Concrete example:** “Everyone who is trained has a badge.” This is a relationship between being trained and having a badge. It is not merely a policy about what people ought to do. It says that every trained person actually has a badge.\n\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\n\nLet **T** mean “is trained” and **B** mean “has a badge.” We can write the rule as **T → B**. The arrow points from the starting condition to the required result. If T is true of someone, B must be true of that same person.\n\nBeing trained is **sufficient** to establish that someone has a badge: it is enough to trigger this rule. Having a badge is **necessary** for being trained under this rule: a trained person cannot lack a badge.\n\nThat does not mean training is the only way to get a badge. It also does not establish that anyone is trained. The rule could hold even if no one met its starting condition.\n\n### Rules can connect different kinds of claims\n\n'''+sections[2].split('### Keep the kind of claim intact\\n\\n',1)[1]\napply='''**Definition:** Applying a rule means finding an established fact that satisfies the rule's starting condition, then concluding the required result for **the same case**.\n\n### Apply the rule forward\n\n**Given fact:** Mira is trained.\n\n**Rule:** Everyone who is trained has a badge.\n\n**Match:** “Mira is trained” satisfies the rule's condition, “is trained.”\n\n**Therefore:** Mira has a badge.\n\n**Why:** The rule covers every trained person, including Mira. We have proved a new fact about Mira. We have not made a claim about everyone who has a badge.\n\n<!-- APPLY-SEQUENCE -->\n\n'''+checks[2]+'''\n\n### When the fact denies the result\n\nA different fact calls for a different move. Suppose **Niko does not have a badge**, and the same rule says **everyone who is trained has a badge**.\n\nIf Niko were trained, the rule would require Niko to have a badge. That would contradict the given fact. So **Niko is not trained**.\n\n**Given fact:** Niko does not have a badge.\n\n**Rule:** Trained → Has a badge.\n\n**Match:** The fact denies the required result. Restate the rule as **No badge → Not trained**.\n\n**Therefore:** Niko is not trained.\n\n**Why:** Training cannot be present while the rule's required result is absent. This is the **contrapositive**: reverse the arrow and negate both complete conditions.\n\nThe contrapositive states the same rule. It does not deny the original rule, and it is not the unsupported reversal **Has a badge → Trained**.\n\nKeep qualifiers inside the conditions. For **Damaged → Should be inspected**, the contrapositive starts with “it is not true that it should be inspected” and concludes “not damaged.” Whether the item has actually been inspected is a different claim.\n\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\n\n### Four facts, one unchanged rule\n\nThe rule is **Trained → Has a badge**. Read what each fact actually matches before choosing a conclusion.\n\n| Given fact about a person | What matches? | What follows? | What remains open, and why? |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| The person is trained (T). | The starting condition. | The person has a badge (B). | Nothing about how other badge holders obtained their badges. |\n| The person has no badge (not B). | The starting condition of the contrapositive. | The person is not trained (not T). | This is the same rule, restated; no badge rules out training. |\n| The person has a badge (B). | Only the original result. | Training is not established. | An untrained visitor with a badge fits the rule. |\n| The person is not trained (not T). | Only the absence of the original starting condition. | Badge status is not established. | An untrained person could have a badge, or could lack one. Both fit the rule. |\n\nThe forbidden combination is **trained, but no badge**. All other combinations can fit the rule. A rule rules out that combination; a fact tells us which condition we actually have.\n\nNow explore those same moves with a different rule: **Every late application requires special approval.** First identify the supplied fact. Then see whether it triggers the original rule, triggers its contrapositive, or leaves the other condition open.\n\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\n\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->\n\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-1 -->\n\n'''+checks[3]+'''\n\nYou do not have to write every contrapositive automatically. If the fact clearly meets the original starting condition, apply that rule. If the fact denies its result, or a connection is difficult to see, a contrapositive can make the supported move clear.\n'''\nlinks='''**Definition:** Linking rules means matching the result of one rule to the starting condition of another, so that together they establish a **new conditional rule**.\n\n### Link the rules without adding a case\n\n**First rule:** Every late application requires special approval.\n\n**Second rule:** Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor's signature.\n\n**Match:** “Requires special approval” is the result of the first rule and the starting condition of the second. The whole idea matches.\n\n**New rule:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor's signature.\n\n**Therefore:** Every late application needs a supervisor's signature.\n\n**Why:** Consider any application that is late. The first rule requires approval; that satisfies the second rule's condition, which requires a signature. This proves the general connection **Late → Needs signature**. It does not establish that any application was late.\n\n### Add a fact and use the whole chain\n\n**Given fact:** Application J was submitted late.\n\n**Rules:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor's signature.\n\n**Therefore:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor's signature.\n\nThe rules-only conclusion was a new **if-then rule**. Adding J's fact lets us apply it and conclude something about **J**. Do not silently add a case when the information gives you only rules.\n\n<!-- TWO-OUTPUTS -->\n\nYou can also use the chain's contrapositive: **Does not need signature → Does not require approval → Was not late**. A fact denying the final result can be carried backward through both connections.\n\n<!-- CHAIN-LAB -->\n\n### The whole middle idea must match\n\n“Requires special approval” and “has received special approval” are not the same condition. A requirement can remain unfulfilled. A rule ending in the first phrase cannot be linked to one starting in the second unless another statement connects them. Shared words are not enough; match the complete claim, including qualifiers and negation.\n\n'''+checks[4]+'''\n\n### A contrapositive can uncover the match\n\nSuppose these two rules are given:\n\n**Rule 1:** Trained → Has a badge.\n\n**Rule 2:** Not trained → Requires supervision.\n\n“Trained” and “not trained” are opposite ideas in the same position: both are starting conditions. They do not look like a chain yet. Take the contrapositive of the first rule:\n\n**Restated rule 1:** No badge → Not trained.\n\nNow “not trained” is the result of that restatement and the starting condition of rule 2. We can link them:\n\n**New chain:** No badge → Not trained → Requires supervision.\n\n**New conditional:** Anyone without a badge requires supervision.\n\nAgain, that is a general rule. It does not prove that someone lacks a badge. If a separate fact says Niko lacks a badge, then we can apply the chain and conclude that Niko requires supervision.\n\n<!-- HIDDEN-CHAIN -->\n\nWhen opposite ideas sit in the same position, try a contrapositive to expose a match. When the match is already visible, use it directly. The point is to find a supported connection, not to produce extra diagrams for their own sake.\n'''\nordered=[('Facts establish information about a case',facts),('Rules connect conditions',rules),('Apply a rule to the same case',apply),('Negate the whole requirement',sections[4]),('Link rules to produce a new rule',links),('Shared beginnings do not create a chain',sections[6]),('Choose the move and test the claim',sections[7]),('Mix the moves',sections[8]),('Take this into your assigned work',sections[9])]\nresult=opening+'\\n\\n'+'\\n\\n'.join(f'## {n:02} | {title}\\n\\n{body.strip()}' for n,(title,body) in enumerate(ordered,1))+'\\n'\nassert result.count('**Try it:**')==7\n(p/'concept-lesson-v6.md').write_text(result,encoding='utf-8')\nb=(p/'build-preview-v5.py').read_text(encoding='utf-8').replace('V5','V6').replace('v5','v6')\n(p/'build-preview-v6.py').write_text(b,encoding='utf-8')\nprint('V6 substantial source rewrite saved; seven checks and four mixed problems preserved.')\n'@ | python -",max_output_tokens:2000}));
